Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Propafenone: (Major) Concomitant use of propafenone and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Proton pump inhibitors: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib capsules or tablets with a proton pump inhibitor (PPI). If therapy that alters gastric pH is required, consider the use of nilotinib oral disintegrating tablets or an H2-blocker or antacid. Separate the administration of nilotinib capsules or tablets and an H2-blocker or antacid: give H2-blockers 10 hours before or 2 hours after nilotinib capsules or tablets, separate antacids from nilotinib capsules or tablets by 2 hours. Nilotinib capsules and tablets display pH-dependent solubility and therapy that raises gastric pH may interfere with nilotinib absorption and reduce nilotinib exposure and efficacy. A PPI has been observed to reduce the absorption of nilotinib capsules and tablets by 34%.
Quazepam: (Moderate) Concomitant use of nilotinib, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, and quazepam, a CYP3A4 substrate, may result in increased quazepam levels. A quazepam dose reduction may be necessary if these drugs are used together.
QUEtiapine: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib with other agents that prolong the QT interval, such as quetiapine. Additionally, nilotinib is a moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4 and quetiapine is a substrate of CYP3A4; administering these drugs together may result in increased quetiapine levels. If the use of quetiapine is necessary, hold nilotinib therapy. If these drugs are used together, consider a quetiapine dose reduction and monitor patients for toxicity (e.g., QT interval prolongation).
quiNIDine: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib with quinidine due to an increased risk for QT prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Systemic exposure of quinidine may be increased resulting in an increase in quinidine-related adverse reactions. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Quinidine is a CYP3A4 substrate that has also been associated with QT prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP).
quiNINE: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and quinine; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. Sudden death and QT prolongation have been reported in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Quinine has been associated with QT prolongation and rare cases of torsade de pointes (TdP).
Quizartinib: (Major) Concomitant use of quizartinib and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
RABEprazole: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib capsules or tablets with a proton pump inhibitor (PPI). If therapy that alters gastric pH is required, consider the use of nilotinib oral disintegrating tablets or an H2-blocker or antacid. Separate the administration of nilotinib capsules or tablets and an H2-blocker or antacid: give H2-blockers 10 hours before or 2 hours after nilotinib capsules or tablets, separate antacids from nilotinib capsules or tablets by 2 hours. Nilotinib capsules and tablets display pH-dependent solubility and therapy that raises gastric pH may interfere with nilotinib absorption and reduce nilotinib exposure and efficacy. A PPI has been observed to reduce the absorption of nilotinib capsules and tablets by 34%.
Ranolazine: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib with ranolazine due to an increased risk for QT prolongation. Additionally, the systemic exposure of ranolazine may be increased resulting in an increase in treatment-related adverse reactions. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor; sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Ranolazine is a CYP3A4 substrate that has also been associated with dose- and plasma concentration-related increases in the QTc interval. Although there are no studies examining the effects of ranolazine in patients receiving other QT prolonging drugs, coadministration of such drugs may result in additive QT prolongation.
Relacorilant: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and relacorilant due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure which may increase the risk for nilotinib-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate and relacorilant is a strong CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
Relugolix: (Major) Avoid administration of nilotinib with other drugs with a known potential to prolong the QT interval such as relugolix. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval.
Relugolix; Estradiol; Norethindrone acetate: (Major) Avoid administration of nilotinib with other drugs with a known potential to prolong the QT interval such as relugolix. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval. (Moderate) Nilotinib is a competitive inhibitor of UGT1A1 and CYP3A4. Estradiol is a substrate of UGT1A1. Increased concentrations of estradiol may occur following coadministration with nilotinib.
Remibrutinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of remibrutinib and nilotinib due to the risk for increased remibrutinib exposure which may increase the risk for remibrutinib-related adverse effects. Remibrutinib is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased remibrutinib overall exposure by 2.3-fold.
Repotrectinib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of repotrectinib with nilotinib due to increased repotrectinib exposure which may increase the risk for repotrectinib-related adverse effects. Repotrectinib is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
Revumenib: (Major) Concomitant use of revumenib and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Ribociclib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and ribociclib due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure, which may increase the risk of nilotinib-related adverse effects, and the additive risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). If concomitant use of ribociclib at a dose of at least 600 mg/day is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. Additionally, consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate, ribociclib at a dose of 600 mg/day is a strong CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
Ribociclib; Letrozole: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and ribociclib due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure, which may increase the risk of nilotinib-related adverse effects, and the additive risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). If concomitant use of ribociclib at a dose of at least 600 mg/day is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. Additionally, consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate, ribociclib at a dose of 600 mg/day is a strong CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
rifAMPin: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and rifampin; significantly decreased nilotinib exposure was observed in a drug interaction study. Coadministration of these drugs may lead to reduced nilotinib efficacy. Nilotinib is a CYPA4 substrate and rifampin is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. In a drug interaction study, the AUC value of nilotinib was decreased by about 80% following the addition of rifampicin/rifampin 600 mg once daily for 12 days.
Rifapentine: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and rifapentine; significantly decreased nilotinib exposure and reduced nilotinib efficacy may occur. Nilotinib is a CYPA4 substrate and rifapentine is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. In a drug interaction study, coadministration with another strong CYP3A4 inducer decreased nilotinib exposure by approximately 80%.
Rilpivirine: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib with other agents that prolong the QT interval, such as rilpivirine. Nilotinib is a moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4 and rilpivirine is a substrate of CYP3A4; administering these drugs together may result in increased rilpivirine levels. If the use of rilpivirine is necessary, hold nilotinib therapy. If these drugs are used together, consider a rilpivirine dose reduction and monitor patients for toxicity (e.g., QT interval prolongation).
Rilzabrutinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of rilzabrutinib and nilotinib due to the risk for increased rilzabrutinib exposure which may increase the risk for rilzabrutinib-related adverse effects. Rilzabrutinib is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor is predicted to increase rilzabrutinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
Rimegepant: (Major) Avoid a second dose of rimegepant within 48 hours if coadministered with nilotinib; concurrent use may increase rimegepant exposure. Rimegepant is a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
risperiDONE: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and risperidone; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. If coadministration is required and the patient has risk factors for cardiac disease or arrhythmias, careful monitoring is recommended. Sudden death and QT prolongation have been reported in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Risperidone has been associated with a possible risk for QT prolongation and/or TdP. Reports of QT prolongation and TdP during risperidone therapy are noted by the manufacturer, primarily in the overdosage setting.
Ritonavir: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and ritonavir due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure which may increase the risk for nilotinib-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate and ritonavir is a strong CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
Rivaroxaban: (Moderate) Patients with renal impairment (CrCl 15 to 79 mL/min) may be at an increased risk for rivaroxaban-related adverse effects such as bleeding and bruising if rivaroxaban and nilotinib are used together. The manufacturer of rivaroxaban recommends avoiding use of dual moderate CYP3A and P-gp inhibitors in patients with renal impairment. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor however the extent to which it inhibits P-gp has not been fully elucidated. In a pharmacokinetic trial, coadministration with a combined moderate CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor increased the AUC of rivaroxaban by 76% in patients with mild renal impairment (CrCl 50 to 79 mL/min) and by 99% in patients with moderate renal impairment (CrCl 30 to 49 mL/min) compared to patients with normal renal function (CrCl greater than 80 mL/min); similar trends in pharmacodynamic effects were also observed.
romiDEPsin: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib with other agents that prolong the QT interval, such as romidepsin. Nilotinib is a moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4 and romidepsin is a substrate of CYP3A4; administering these drugs together may result in increased romidepsin levels. If the use of romidepsin is necessary, hold nilotinib therapy. If these drugs are used together, consider a romidepsin dose reduction and monitor patients for toxicity (e.g., QT interval prolongation).
Rotavirus Vaccine: (Contraindicated) Live virus vaccines should generally not be administered to an immunosuppressed patient. Live virus vaccines may induce the illness they are intended to prevent and are generally contraindicated for use during immunosuppressive treatment. The immune response of the immunocompromised patient to vaccines may be decreased, even despite alternate vaccination schedules or more frequent booster doses. If immunization is necessary, choose an alternative to live vaccination, or, consider a delay or change in the immunization schedule. Practitioners should refer to the most recent CDC guidelines regarding vaccination of patients who are receiving drugs that adversely affect the immune system.
Saquinavir: (Contraindicated) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and saquinavir. Concomitant use increases the risk for torsade de pointes (TdP) and QT/QTc prolongation and may increase the exposure of both drugs and the risk for adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate and moderate CYP3A inhibitor, saquinavir is a CYP3A substrate and strong CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) vaccines: (Moderate) Patients receiving immunosuppressant medications may have a diminished response to the SARS-CoV-2 virus vaccine. When feasible, administer indicated vaccines prior to initiating immunosuppressant medications. Counsel patients receiving immunosuppressant medications about the possibility of a diminished vaccine response and to continue to follow precautions to avoid exposure to SARS-CoV-2 virus after receiving the vaccine.
SARS-CoV-2 Virus (COVID-19) mRNA Vaccine: (Moderate) Patients receiving immunosuppressant medications may have a diminished response to the SARS-CoV-2 virus vaccine. When feasible, administer indicated vaccines prior to initiating immunosuppressant medications. Counsel patients receiving immunosuppressant medications about the possibility of a diminished vaccine response and to continue to follow precautions to avoid exposure to SARS-CoV-2 virus after receiving the vaccine.
SARS-CoV-2 Virus (COVID-19) Recombinant Spike Protein Nanoparticle Vaccine: (Moderate) Patients receiving immunosuppressant medications may have a diminished response to the SARS-CoV-2 virus vaccine. When feasible, administer indicated vaccines prior to initiating immunosuppressant medications. Counsel patients receiving immunosuppressant medications about the possibility of a diminished vaccine response and to continue to follow precautions to avoid exposure to SARS-CoV-2 virus after receiving the vaccine.
Sebetralstat: (Major) Decrease the sebetralstat dose by 50% if concomitant use with nilotinib is necessary. Concomitant use may increase sebetralstat exposure and the risk for sebetralstat-related adverse effects. Sebetralstat is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased sebetralstat overall exposure by 2-fold.
Segesterone Acetate; Ethinyl Estradiol: (Minor) Coadministration of segesterone, a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor may increase the serum concentration of segesterone.
Seladelpar: (Minor) Monitor patients who are known CYP2C9 poor metabolizers more frequently for an increase in seladelpar-related adverse effects if concomitant use with nilotinib is necessary. Concomitant use in these patients may increase seladelpar exposure. Seladelpar is a CYP2C9 and CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
Selpercatinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of selpercatinib and nilotinib due to the potential for increased selpercatinib exposure and additive risk for QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). If coadministration is necessary, reduce the dose of selpercatinib (160 mg twice daily to 120 mg twice daily; 120 mg twice daily to 80 mg twice daily; 80 mg twice daily to 40 mg twice daily; 40 mg three times daily to 40 mg once daily). Additionally, consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring. Selpercatinib is a CYP3A substrate, nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT interval prolongation. Coadministration with other moderate CYP3A inhibitors is predicted to increase selpercatinib exposure by 1.66-fold to 1.99-fold.
Selumetinib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of selumetinib and nilotinib due to the risk of increased selumetinib exposure which may increase the risk of adverse reactions. If coadministration is unavoidable, reduce the dose of selumetinib to 20 mg/m2 PO twice daily if original dose was 25 mg/m2 twice daily and 15 mg/m2 PO twice daily if original dose was 20 mg/m2 twice daily. If nilotinib is discontinued, resume the original selumetinib dose after 3 elimination half-lives of nilotinib. Selumetinib is a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Coadministration with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor is predicted to increase selumetinib exposure by 41%.
Sertraline: (Major) Concomitant use of sertraline and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary. The degree of QT prolongation associated with sertraline is not clinically significant when administered within the recommended dosage range; QT prolongation has been described at 2 times the maximum recommended dose.
Sevabertinib: (Moderate) Monitor for sevabertinib-related adverse effects if concomitant use with nilotinib is necessary. Concomitant use may increase sevabertinib exposure. Sevabertinib is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
Sevoflurane: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and halogenated anesthetics; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. Sudden death and QT prolongation have been reported in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Additionally, fatal cases of torsade de pointes have been reported with halogenated anesthetic use.
Sildenafil: (Moderate) Monitor for an increase in sildenafil-related adverse reactions if coadministration with nilotinib is necessary; a dose reduction of sildenafil may be necessary when prescribed for erectile dysfunction. Sildenafil is a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
Siponimod: (Major) In general, do not initiate treatment with siponimod in patients receiving nilotinib due to the potential for QT prolongation. Consult a cardiologist regarding appropriate monitoring if siponimod use is required. Siponimod therapy prolonged the QT interval at recommended doses in a clinical study. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Additionally, concomitant use of siponimod and nilotinib may increase siponimod exposure. If the patient is also receiving a drug regimen containing a moderate CYP2C9 inhibitor, use of siponimod is not recommended due to a significant increase in siponimod exposure. Siponimod is a CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 substrate; nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Coadministration with a moderate CYP2C9/CYP3A4 dual inhibitor led to a 2-fold increase in the exposure of siponimod.
Sirolimus: (Moderate) Monitor sirolimus concentrations and adjust sirolimus dosage as appropriate during concomitant use of nilotinib. Coadministration may increase sirolimus concentrations and increase the risk for sirolimus-related adverse effects. Sirolimus is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased sirolimus overall exposure 1.6-fold.
Smallpox and Monkeypox Vaccine, Live, Nonreplicating: (Contraindicated) Live virus vaccines should generally not be administered to an immunosuppressed patient. Live virus vaccines may induce the illness they are intended to prevent and are generally contraindicated for use during immunosuppressive treatment. The immune response of the immunocompromised patient to vaccines may be decreased, even despite alternate vaccination schedules or more frequent booster doses. If immunization is necessary, choose an alternative to live vaccination, or, consider a delay or change in the immunization schedule. Practitioners should refer to the most recent CDC guidelines regarding vaccination of patients who are receiving drugs that adversely affect the immune system.
Smallpox and Mpox (Vaccinia) Vaccine, Live: (Contraindicated) Live virus vaccines should generally not be administered to an immunosuppressed patient. Live virus vaccines may induce the illness they are intended to prevent and are generally contraindicated for use during immunosuppressive treatment. The immune response of the immunocompromised patient to vaccines may be decreased, even despite alternate vaccination schedules or more frequent booster doses. If immunization is necessary, choose an alternative to live vaccination, or, consider a delay or change in the immunization schedule. Practitioners should refer to the most recent CDC guidelines regarding vaccination of patients who are receiving drugs that adversely affect the immune system.
Sodium Bicarbonate: (Moderate) If therapy that alters gastric pH is required, consider the use of nilotinib oral disintegrating tablets or separate the administration of an antacid and nilotinib capsules or tablets by at least 2 hours. Nilotinib capsules and tablets display pH-dependent solubility and therapy that raises gastric pH may interfere with nilotinib absorption and reduce nilotinib exposure and efficacy.
Sofosbuvir; Velpatasvir: (Moderate) Use caution when administering velpatasvir with nilotinib. Taking these medications together may increase the plasma concentrations of velpatasvir, potentially resulting in adverse events. Nilotinib is a moderate inhibitor of the hepatic enzyme CYP3A4. Velpatasvir is a CYP3A4 substrate.
Sofosbuvir; Velpatasvir; Voxilaprevir: (Moderate) Use caution when administering velpatasvir with nilotinib. Taking these medications together may increase the plasma concentrations of velpatasvir, potentially resulting in adverse events. Nilotinib is a moderate inhibitor of the hepatic enzyme CYP3A4. Velpatasvir is a CYP3A4 substrate.
Sonidegib: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of sonidegib and nilotinib; sonidegib exposure may be significantly increased resulting in increased risk of adverse events, particularly musculoskeletal toxicity. Sonidegib is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Physiologic-based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) simulations indicate a moderate 3A4 inhibitor would increase the sonidegib AUC by 1.8-fold if administered for 14 days and by 2.8-fold if the moderate CYP3A inhibitor is administered with sonidegib for more than 14 days.
Sonrotoclax: (Major) A sonrotoclax dosage decrease is required if concomitant use with nilotinib is necessary. During the sonrotoclax initiation and ramp-up phase, reduce the sonrotoclax dose by at least 4-fold; avoid concomitant use when the sonrotoclax dose is 2 mg or less. After the sonrotoclax ramp-up phase, reduce the target daily dose of sonrotoclax to 80 mg. Concomitant use may increase sonrotoclax exposure which may increase the risk for sonrotoclax-related adverse effects. Sonrotoclax is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
SORAfenib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib with sorafenib due to the risk of QT prolongation. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Sorafenib has also been associated with QT prolongation.
Sotalol: (Major) Concomitant use of sotalol and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Sparsentan: (Moderate) Monitor for an increase in sparsentan-related adverse effects if concomitant use with nilotinib is necessary. Concomitant use may increase sparsentan exposure. Sparsentan is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased sparsentan overall exposure by 70%.
St. John's Wort, Hypericum perforatum: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and St. John's Wort; significantly decreased nilotinib exposure and reduced nilotinib efficacy may occur. Nilotinib is a CYPA4 substrate and St. John's Wort is a strong CYP3A4 inducer. In a drug interaction study, coadministration with another strong CYP3A4 inducer decreased nilotinib exposure by approximately 80%.
SUFentanil: (Major) Because the dose of the sufentanil sublingual tablets cannot be titrated, consider an alternate opiate if nilotinib must be administered. Consider a reduced dose of sufentanil injection with frequent monitoring for respiratory depression and sedation if concurrent use of nilotinib is necessary. If nilotinib is discontinued, consider increasing the sufentanil injection dose until stable drug effects are achieved and monitor for evidence of opioid withdrawal. Sufentanil is a CYP3A4 substrate, and coadministration with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor like nilotinib can increase sufentanil exposure resulting in increased or prolonged opioid effects including fatal respiratory depression, particularly when an inhibitor is added to a stable dose of sufentanil. If nilotinib is discontinued, sufentanil plasma concentrations will decrease resulting in reduced efficacy of the opioid and potential withdrawal syndrome in a patient who has developed physical dependence to sufentanil.
SUNItinib: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and sunitinib; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. Sudden death and QT prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Sunitinib can cause dose-dependent QT prolongation, which may increase the risk for ventricular arrhythmias, including torsades de points.
Suvorexant: (Major) A dose reduction to 5 mg of suvorexant is recommended during concurrent use with nilotinib. The suvorexant dose may be increased to 10 mg if needed for efficacy. Suvorexant is a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Coadministration with a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor increased the suvorexant AUC by 2-fold.
Suzetrigine: (Major) A suzetrigine dosage reduction is required during concomitant use of nilotinib. Beginning with dose 5 and for subsequent doses, administer suzetrigine 50 mg once daily. Concomitant use may increase suzetrigine exposure and the risk for suzetrigine-related adverse effects. Suzetrigine and its active metabolite M6-SUZ are CYP3A substrates and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. When the recommended dosage reduction is applied, concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor is predicted to increase suzetrigine and M6-SUZ overall exposure by 1.5- and 1.2-fold, respectively.
Tacrolimus: (Major) Nilotinib and tacrolimus both prolong the QT interval. Additionally, tacrolimus is metabolized by CYP3A4 and nilotinib inhibits this isoenzyme. Coadministration of nilotinib and a drug that prolongs the QT interval is not advised, as nilotinib prolongs the QT interval. If concurrent administration with tacrolimus is unavoidable, the manufacturer of nilotinib recommends interruption of nilotinib treatment. If nilotinib must be continued, closely monitor the patient for QT interval prolongation. The manufacturer of tacrolimus advises reducing the tacrolimus dose, close monitoring of tacrolimus whole blood concentrations, and monitoring for QT prolongation when coadministrating tacrolimus with other substrates and/or inhibitors of CYP3A4 that also have the potential to prolong the QT interval such as nilotinib. Tacrolimus concentrations and thus other adverse reactions may also be increased during concomitant administration.
Tadalafil: (Moderate) Concomitant use of nilotinib, an moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, and tadalafil, a CYP3A4 substrate, may result in increased tadalafil levels. A tadalafil dose reduction may be necessary if these drugs are used together.
Taletrectinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of taletrectinib and nilotinib due to the risk for increased taletrectinib exposure and additive risk for QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Taletrectinib is a CYP3A substrate, nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with other moderate CYP3A inhibitors is predicted to increase taletrectinib overall exposure by up to 2.6-fold.
Tamoxifen: (Major) Concomitant use of tamoxifen and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Tasimelteon: (Moderate) Caution is recommended during concurrent use of tasimelteon and nilotinib. Because tasimelteon is partially metabolized via CYP3A4, use with CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as nilotinib, may increase exposure to tasimelteon with the potential for adverse reactions.
Tazemetostat: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of tazemetostat with nilotinib due to the risk for increased tazemetostat exposure which may increase the risk for adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, decrease current tazemetostat daily dosage by 50% (e.g., decrease 800 mg PO twice daily to 400 mg PO twice daily; 600 mg PO twice daily to 400 mg PO for first dose and 200 mg PO for second dose; 400 mg PO twice daily to 200 mg PO twice daily). Tazemetostat is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Coadministration with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased tazemetostat overall exposure by 3.1-fold.
Telavancin: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and telavancin; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. Sudden death and QT prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Telavancin has been associated with QT prolongation.
Telmisartan; amLODIPine: (Moderate) Coadministration of CYP3A4 inhibitors with amlodipine can theoretically decrease the hepatic metabolism of amlodipine (a CYP3A4 substrate). Caution should be used when CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as nilotinib, are coadministered with calcium-channel blockers. Monitor therapeutic response; a dose reduction of amlodipine may be required.
Telmisartan; amLODIPine; Indapamide: (Moderate) Coadministration of CYP3A4 inhibitors with amlodipine can theoretically decrease the hepatic metabolism of amlodipine (a CYP3A4 substrate). Caution should be used when CYP3A4 inhibitors, such as nilotinib, are coadministered with calcium-channel blockers. Monitor therapeutic response; a dose reduction of amlodipine may be required.
Tetrabenazine: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and tetrabenazine; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. Sudden death and QT prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Tetrabenazine causes a small increase in the corrected QT interval.
Tezacaftor; Ivacaftor: (Major) Adjust the tezacaftor; ivacaftor dosing schedule when coadministered with nilotinib; coadministration may increase tezacaftor; ivacaftor exposure and adverse reactions. When combined, dose 1 tezacaftor; ivacaftor combination tablet every other day in the morning and 1 ivacaftor tablet every other day in the morning on alternate days (i.e., tezacaftor/ivacaftor tablet on Day 1 and ivacaftor tablet on Day 2). The evening dose of ivacaftor should not be taken. Both tezacaftor and ivacaftor are CYP3A substrates (ivacaftor is a sensitive substrate); nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Coadministration of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased ivacaftor exposure 3-fold. Simulation suggests a moderate inhibitor may increase tezacaftor exposure 2-fold. (Major) If nilotinib and ivacaftor are taken together, administer ivacaftor at the usual recommended dose but reduce the frequency to once daily. Coadministration is not recommended in patients younger than 6 months. Ivacaftor is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Coadministration with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased ivacaftor exposure by 3-fold.
Thioridazine: (Contraindicated) Nilotinib prolongs the QT interval. Because of the potential for torsade de pointes, use of thioridazine with nilotinib is contraindicated.
Ticagrelor: (Moderate) Monitor for increased bleeding if ticagrelor is coadministered with nilotinib. Coadministration may increase the exposure of ticagrelor. Ticagrelor is a sensitive substrate of CYP3A; nilotinib is a moderate inhibitor of CYP3A.
Tipranavir: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and tipranavir due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure which may increase the risk for nilotinib-related adverse effects. The exposure of tipranavir may also be increased. If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate and moderate CYP3A inhibitor and tipranavir is a CYP3A substrate and strong CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
Tolterodine: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib with tolterodine due to an increased risk for QT prolongation. Systemic exposure of tolterodine may also be increased resulting in increase in treatment-related adverse reactions. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor; sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Tolterodine is a CYP3A4 substrate that has also been associated with dose-dependent prolongation of the QT interval, especially in poor CYP2D6 metabolizers. Concomitant use may increase the risk for QT prolongation.
Tolvaptan: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib when tolvaptan is administered for hyponatremia. In patients with autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD), reduce tolvaptan dosage if administered with nilotinib. In ADPKD patients receiving tolvaptan 90mg every morning and 30 mg every evening, reduce the dose to 45 mg every morning and 15 mg every evening; for those receiving tolvaptan 60 mg every morning and 30 mg every evening, reduce the dose to 30 mg every morning and 15 mg every evening; for those receiving tolvaptan 45 mg every morning and 15 mg every evening, reduce the dose to 15 mg every morning and 15 mg every evening. Consider additional dosage reduction if the reduced dose is not tolerated. Tolvaptan is a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate; nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Coadministration of another moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor increased the tolvaptan AUC by 200%.
Toremifene: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib with toremifene due to the risk of additive QT prolongation. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Toremifene has also been shown to prolong the QTc interval in a dose- and concentration-related manner.
traMADol: (Major) The concomitant use of nilotinib and tramadol may reduce the clearance of tramadol and increase the risk for serious adverse events including seizures and serotonin syndrome. If coadministration of these drugs is required, consider reducing the dose of tramadol and monitor patients for signs of toxicity. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor and tramadol is a CYP3A4 substrate.
Tramadol; Acetaminophen: (Major) The concomitant use of nilotinib and tramadol may reduce the clearance of tramadol and increase the risk for serious adverse events including seizures and serotonin syndrome. If coadministration of these drugs is required, consider reducing the dose of tramadol and monitor patients for signs of toxicity. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor and tramadol is a CYP3A4 substrate.
Trandolapril; Verapamil: (Moderate) Monitor blood pressure and heart rate if coadministration of verapamil with nilotinib is necessary; also monitor for nilotinib-related side effects. Nilotinib and verapamil are both substrates of and inhibitors of CYP3A4. Elevations of verapamil plasma levels resulting in clinically significant interactions have been reported with other moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors. Increased nilotinib concentrations may increase the risk for treatment-related side effects.
traZODone: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib with other agents that prolong the QT interval. Trazodone can prolong the QT interval at therapeutic doses, and torsade de pointes (TdP) has been reported with post-marketing use. Additionally, nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor and trazodone is a CYP3A4 substrate; administering these drugs together may result in increased trazodone levels. If the use of trazodone is required, hold nilotinib therapy. If the use of nilotinib and trazodone cannot be avoided, a trazodone dose reduction may be necessary; close monitoring of the QT interval is recommended.
Tretinoin, ATRA: (Moderate) Monitor for an increase in tretinoin-related adverse effects if concomitant use with nilotinib is necessary. Concomitant use may increase tretinoin exposure. Tretinoin is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
Triazolam: (Moderate) Monitor for signs of triazolam toxicity during coadministration with nilotinib and consider appropriate dose reduction of triazolam if clinically indicated. Coadministration may increase triazolam exposure. Triazolam is a sensitive CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
Triclabendazole: (Major) Concomitant use of triclabendazole and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Trifluoperazine: (Minor) QT interval prolongation may be additive if nilotinib and trifluoperazine are coadministered. Sudden death and QT prolongation have been reported in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Trifluoperazine is associated with a possible risk of QT prolongation.
Triptorelin: (Major) Avoid administration of nilotinib with triptorelin due to the risk of QT interval prolongation. Sudden death and QT prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., triptorelin) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval.
Tucatinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and tucatinib due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure which may increase the risk for nilotinib-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate and tucatinib is a strong CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold.
Typhoid Vaccine: (Contraindicated) Live virus vaccines should generally not be administered to an immunosuppressed patient. Live virus vaccines may induce the illness they are intended to prevent and are generally contraindicated for use during immunosuppressive treatment. The immune response of the immunocompromised patient to vaccines may be decreased, even despite alternate vaccination schedules or more frequent booster doses. If immunization is necessary, choose an alternative to live vaccination, or, consider a delay or change in the immunization schedule. Practitioners should refer to the most recent CDC guidelines regarding vaccination of patients who are receiving drugs that adversely affect the immune system.
Ubrogepant: (Major) Limit the initial dose of ubrogepant to 50 mg and avoid a second dose within 24 hours if coadministered with nilotinib. Concurrent use may increase ubrogepant exposure and the risk of adverse effects. Ubrogepant is a CYP3A4 substrate; nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Coadministration with another moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor resulted in a 3.5-fold increase in the exposure of ubrogepant.
Vandetanib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of vandetanib with nilotinib due to an increased risk of QT prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Vandetanib can prolong the QT interval in a concentration-dependent manner; TdP and sudden death have been reported in patients receiving vandetanib. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy.
Vanzacaftor; Tezacaftor; Deutivacaftor: (Major) Adjust the tezacaftor; ivacaftor dosing schedule when coadministered with nilotinib; coadministration may increase tezacaftor; ivacaftor exposure and adverse reactions. When combined, dose 1 tezacaftor; ivacaftor combination tablet every other day in the morning and 1 ivacaftor tablet every other day in the morning on alternate days (i.e., tezacaftor/ivacaftor tablet on Day 1 and ivacaftor tablet on Day 2). The evening dose of ivacaftor should not be taken. Both tezacaftor and ivacaftor are CYP3A substrates (ivacaftor is a sensitive substrate); nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Coadministration of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased ivacaftor exposure 3-fold. Simulation suggests a moderate inhibitor may increase tezacaftor exposure 2-fold. (Major) Decrease the vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor dosage during concomitant use of nilotinib; concomitant use may increase vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor exposure and the risk for vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor-related adverse effects. Specific dosing recommendations vary by age and weight. For all patients 12 years and older, and patients younger than 12 years weighing at least 40 kg, reduce the dose to 1 tablet of vanzacaftor 10 mg; tezacaftor 50 mg; deutivacaftor 125 mg every other day. For patients younger than 12 years weighing less than 40 kg, reduce the dose to 2 tablets of vanzacaftor 4 mg; tezacaftor 20 mg; deutivacaftor 50 mg every other day. Vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased vanzacaftor and deutivacaftor overall exposure by 3.9- and 5.1-fold, respectively.
Vanzacaftor; Tezacaftor; Deutivacaftor: (Major) Decrease the vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor dosage during concomitant use of nilotinib; concomitant use may increase vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor exposure and the risk for vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor-related adverse effects. Specific dosing recommendations vary by age and weight. For all patients 12 years and older, and patients younger than 12 years weighing at least 40 kg, reduce the dose to 1 tablet of vanzacaftor 10 mg; tezacaftor 50 mg; deutivacaftor 125 mg every other day. For patients younger than 12 years weighing less than 40 kg, reduce the dose to 2 tablets of vanzacaftor 4 mg; tezacaftor 20 mg; deutivacaftor 50 mg every other day. Vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased vanzacaftor and deutivacaftor overall exposure by 3.9- and 5.1-fold, respectively.
Vardenafil: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib with other agents known to prolong the QT interval, such as vardenafil. Sudden death and QT prolongation have occurred in patients receiving nilotinib therapy. Both therapeutic and supratherapeutic doses of vardenafil can produce an increase in QTc interval. Also, nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor and vardenafil is a primary CYP3A4 substrate; administering these drugs together may result in increased vardenafil concentrations. Coadministration with another moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor increased the AUC of vardenafil by 4-fold. Do not use vardenafil orally disintegrating tablets with nilotinib due to increased vardenafil exposure. If use of nilotinib and vardenafil oral tablets is required, do not exceed a single vardenafil oral tablet dose of 5 mg per 24-hour period.
Varicella Virus Vaccine, Live: (Contraindicated) Live virus vaccines should generally not be administered to an immunosuppressed patient. Live virus vaccines may induce the illness they are intended to prevent and are generally contraindicated for use during immunosuppressive treatment. The immune response of the immunocompromised patient to vaccines may be decreased, even despite alternate vaccination schedules or more frequent booster doses. If immunization is necessary, choose an alternative to live vaccination, or, consider a delay or change in the immunization schedule. Practitioners should refer to the most recent CDC guidelines regarding vaccination of patients who are receiving drugs that adversely affect the immune system.
Vemurafenib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of vemurafenib with nilotinib with vemurafenib due to the risk of QT prolongation. Sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Vemurafenib has also been associated with QT prolongation. Additive QT prolongation may occur.
Venetoclax: (Major) Reduce the dose of venetoclax by at least 50% and monitor for venetoclax toxicity (e.g., hematologic toxicity, GI toxicity, and tumor lysis syndrome) if coadministered with nilotinib due to the potential for increased venetoclax exposure. Resume the original venetoclax dose 2 to 3 days after discontinuation of nilotinib. Venetoclax is a CYP3A4 substrate; nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
Venlafaxine: (Major) Avoid coadministration of nilotinib with venlafaxine due to an increased risk for QT prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Systemic exposure of venlafaxine may also be increased resulting in increase in treatment-related adverse reactions. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor; sudden death and QT interval prolongation have occurred in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Venlafaxine is a CYP3A4 substrate that is also associated with a possible risk of QT prolongation; TdP has reported with postmarketing use. Concomitant use may increase the risk for QT prolongation.
Vepdegestrant: (Major) Concomitant use of vepdegestrant and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Verapamil: (Moderate) Monitor blood pressure and heart rate if coadministration of verapamil with nilotinib is necessary; also monitor for nilotinib-related side effects. Nilotinib and verapamil are both substrates of and inhibitors of CYP3A4. Elevations of verapamil plasma levels resulting in clinically significant interactions have been reported with other moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors. Increased nilotinib concentrations may increase the risk for treatment-related side effects.
vinBLAStine: (Moderate) Monitor for an earlier onset and/or increased severity of vinblastine-related adverse reactions, including myelosuppression, constipation, and peripheral neuropathy, if coadministration with nilotinib is necessary. Vinblastine is a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. Enhanced vinblastine toxicity was reported with coadministration of another moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
vinCRIStine Liposomal: (Moderate) Monitor for increased severity or earlier onset of vincristine-related adverse reactions (e.g., periipheral, autonomic and central neuropathy; low blood counts) if coadministration with nilotinib is necessary. Nilotinib may increase vincristine exposure. Vincristine is a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
vinCRIStine: (Moderate) Monitor for increased severity or earlier onset of vincristine-related adverse reactions (e.g., periipheral, autonomic and central neuropathy; low blood counts) if coadministration with nilotinib is necessary. Nilotinib may increase vincristine exposure. Vincristine is a CYP3A4 substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
Vinorelbine: (Moderate) Monitor for an earlier onset and/or increased severity of vinorelbine-related adverse reactions, including constipation and peripheral neuropathy, if coadministration with nilotinib is necessary. Vinorelbine is a CYP3A substrate and nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
Voclosporin: (Major) Avoid coadministration of voclosporin and nilotinib due to the risk of QT prolongation. Voclosporin exposure and the risk for voclosporin-related adverse effects may also be increased. If concomitant use is necessary, reduce the voclosporin dosage to 15.8 mg PO in the morning and 7.9 mg PO in the evening. Voclosporin is a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate that is associated with QT prolongation at supratherapeutic doses. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor that has been associated with sudden death and QT prolongation. Coadministration with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors is predicted to increase voclosporin exposure by 3-fold.
Vonoprazan: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and vonoprazan. Vonoprazan reduces intragastric acidity, which may decrease the absorption of nilotinib reducing its efficacy.
Vonoprazan; Amoxicillin: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and vonoprazan. Vonoprazan reduces intragastric acidity, which may decrease the absorption of nilotinib reducing its efficacy.
Vonoprazan; Amoxicillin; Clarithromycin: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and clarithromycin due to the risk for increased nilotinib exposure, which may increase the risk of nilotinib-related adverse effects, and the additive risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. Additionally, consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate, clarithromycin is a strong CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold. (Major) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and vonoprazan. Vonoprazan reduces intragastric acidity, which may decrease the absorption of nilotinib reducing its efficacy.
Voriconazole: (Contraindicated) Avoid concomitant use of nilotinib and voriconazole. Concomitant use increases the risk for torsade de pointes (TdP) and QT/QTc prolongation and may increase the exposure of both drugs and the risk for adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, a nilotinib dosage reduction is required; specific management recommendations vary by indication and dosage form. For patients receiving nilotinib for resistant or intolerant Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 300 mg for capsules, 142 mg for tablets, 120 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. For patients receiving nilotinib for newly diagnosed Ph+ CML, reduce the nilotinib once daily dose to: 200 mg for capsules, 95 mg for tablets, 80 mg for oral disintegrating tablets. Nilotinib is a CYP3A substrate and moderate CYP3A inhibitor, voriconazole is a CYP3A substrate and strong CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased nilotinib overall exposure by 3-fold. Coadministration with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor increased voriconazole exposure by 79%.
Vorinostat: (Major) Avoid the concomitant use of nilotinib and vorinotat; significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur. Sudden death and QT prolongation have been reported in patients who received nilotinib therapy. Vorinostat therapy is associated with a risk of QT prolongation.
Warfarin: (Moderate) Closely monitor the INR if coadministration of warfarin with nilotinib is necessary as concurrent use may increase the exposure of warfarin leading to increased bleeding risk. Nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor and the R-enantiomer of warfarin is a CYP3A4 substrate. The S-enantiomer of warfarin exhibits 2 to 5 times more anticoagulant activity than the R-enantiomer, but the R-enantiomer generally has a slower clearance.
Yellow Fever Vaccine, Live: (Contraindicated) Live virus vaccines should generally not be administered to an immunosuppressed patient. Live virus vaccines may induce the illness they are intended to prevent and are generally contraindicated for use during immunosuppressive treatment. The immune response of the immunocompromised patient to vaccines may be decreased, even despite alternate vaccination schedules or more frequent booster doses. If immunization is necessary, choose an alternative to live vaccination, or, consider a delay or change in the immunization schedule. Practitioners should refer to the most recent CDC guidelines regarding vaccination of patients who are receiving drugs that adversely affect the immune system.
Zafirlukast: (Major) The concomitant use of nilotinib, a substrate and inhibitor of CYP3A4, and zafirlukast, a CYP3A4 inhibitor, may result in increased nilotinib levels. Monitor patients for nilotinib toxicity (e.g., QT interval prolongation) if these drugs are used together.
Zanubrutinib: (Major) Decrease the zanubrutinib dose to 80 mg PO twice daily if coadministered with nilotinib. Coadministration may result in increased zanubrutinib exposure and toxicity (e.g., infection, bleeding, and atrial arrhythmias). Further decrease the zanubrutinib dose as recommended if adverse reactions occur. After discontinuation of nilotinib, resume the previous dose of zanubrutinib. Zanubrutinib is a CYP3A4 substrate; nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor. The AUC of zanubrutinib is predicted to increase by 157% to 317% when coadministered with other moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors.
Zidesamtinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of zidesamtinib and nilotinib due to the risk for increased zidesamtinib exposure, which may increase the risk for zidesamtinib-related adverse effects, and the additive risk for QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Zidesamtinib is a CYP3A substrate, nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation.
Ziftomenib: (Major) Concomitant use of ziftomenib and nilotinib increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) and may increase ziftomenib exposure and the risk for other adverse effects. Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary. Ziftomenib is a CYP3A substrate, nilotinib is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation. Concomitant use with another moderate CYP3A inhibitor is estimated to increase ziftomenib overall exposure by 2-fold.
Ziprasidone: (Contraindicated) Ziprasidone is contraindicated with any drugs that list QT prolongation as a pharmacodynamic effect when this effect has been described within the contraindications or bolded or boxed warnings of the official labeling for such drugs, including nilotinib. Ziprasidone has been associated with a possible risk for QT prolongation and/or torsade de pointes (TdP). Nilotinib is is associated with concentration-dependent QT prolongation. Significant prolongation of the QT interval may occur when nilotinib is given with medications with a known potential to prolong the QT interval, including ziprasidone.
Zolpidem: (Moderate) It is advisable to closely monitor zolpidem tolerability and safety during concurrent use of nilotinib, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, since CYP3A4 is the primary isoenzyme responsible for zolpidem metabolism. There is evidence of an increase in pharmacodynamics effects and systemic exposure of zolpidem when the drug is co-administered with some potent inhibitors of CYP3A4, such as azole antifungals. Concurrent administration of nilotinib and midazolam, a CYP3A4 substrate, increased midazolam exposure by 30%.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  NOTE: Temporary or permanent discontinuation of nilotinib is needed for clinically significant moderate or severe non-hematologic toxicity (see Dosage).
 
Pancytopenia was reported in 1—10% of patients in clinical trials, and a common adverse effect of nilotinib is myelosuppression. Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, 11% had grade 3 thrombocytopenia, 17% had grade 4 thrombocytopenia, 28% had grade 3 or 4 neutropenia, and 8% had had grade 3 or 4 anemia. Grade 3 or 4 cytopenias were also common among 120 patients with accelerated phase CML: 7% had grade 3 thrombocytopenia, 30% had grade 4 thrombocytopenia, 12% had grade 3 neutropenia, 25% had grade 4 neutropenia, and 23% had had grade 3 or 4 anemia. Myelosuppression was generally reversible and usually managed by temporary suspension of nilotinib or dose reduction of nilotinib. Suspension of nilotinib and dose reduction may be needed for neutropenia or thrombocytopenia (see Dosage). In a study of 280 patients with chronic phase CML, the median duration of neutropenia was 15 days, and the median duration of thrombocytopenia was 22 days. Dose interruption and reduction were required in 10% of patients with neutropenia and in 19% of patients with thrombocytopenia. Support with hematopoietic growth factors was needed in 5% of patients, and platelet transfusions were needed by 10% of patients. Obtain a complete blood count every 2 weeks for the first 2 months. Thereafter, obtain a complete blood count every month, or more frequently if clinically indicated.
 
Nausea/vomiting, diarrhea, constipation, and abdominal pain are common adverse effects of nilotinib. Nausea occurred in 31%, vomiting in 21%, diarrhea in 22%, constipation in 21%, and abdominal pain in 11% of 318 patients with chronic phase CML. Among 120 patients with accelerated phase CML, 18% had nausea, 19% had diarrhea, 18% had constipation, 10% had vomiting, and 13% had abdominal pain. Other GI effects that occurred in 1—10% of patients include dyspepsia and anorexia. Gastrointestinal effects can be severe. For example, grade 3 or 4 severity diarrhea occurred in 3% of chronic phase patients and in 2% of accelerated phase patients. Elevated hepatic enzymes of grade 3 or 4 severity were seen in 1—4% of patients. Grade 3 or 4 hyperbilirubinemia (9—10%) and hyperamylasemia (14%) also occurred. In a pharmacogenetic analysis, patients with the (TA)7/(TA)7 genotype had a statistically significant increase in the risk of hyperbilirubinemia relative to the (TA)6/(TA)6 and (TA)6/(TA)7 genotypes. The largest increases in bilirubin were observed in the (TA)7/(TA)7 genotype (UGT1A1*28) patients. Nilotinib may also cause hypoalbuminemia and increases in alkaline phosphatase and in serum lipase concentrations. In a study of 280 patients with chronic-phase CML, grade 3 or 4 elevations in serum lipase were seen in 14% of patients, and 1% developed pancreatitis. Lipase elevations were generally self-limiting and resolved spontaneously with continued dosing at the same or reduced dose within 1—2 weeks. Fifteen percent of 318 patients with chronic phase CML and 17% of 120 patients with accelerated phase CML had grade 3 or 4 elevated lipase concentrations. Hepatic enzymes, serum amylase, bilirubin, and serum lipase should be monitored periodically during therapy. Nilotinib discontinuation or dose adjustment may be needed (see Dosage). Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, 11% had grade 3 or 4 hyperglycemia whereas 4% of 120 patients with accelerated phase CML had the event.
 
Hypertension and palpitations were noted in 1—10% of patients receiving nilotinib in clinical trials. Nilotinib has been shown to cause QT prolongation in a concentration-dependent manner. QT prolongation may cause torsade de pointes, although no episodes of torsade de pointes were observed in clinical studies. Among healthy patients, the maximum mean placebo-adjusted QTcF change from baseline was 18 msec, and the upper 95% CI was 26 msec. Of note, the peak plasma nilotinib concentrations were 26% lower in the QT trial than the concentrations observed in the clinical trial. Among 337 patients with imatinib-resistant or intolerant CML, the maximum mean QTcF change from baseline at steady state was 10 msec. A greater than 60 msec increase from baseline was observed in 2.1% of patients, and 500 msec. Sudden deaths have occurred in 5 of 867 patients in an on-going study. The early occurrence of these deaths in relation to initiation of nilotinib are suggestive of ventricular repolarization abnormalities. Nilotinib may cause hypokalemia, hyperkalemia, hypocalcemia, hyponatremia, hypomagnesemia, and hypophosphatemia. Among patients with either chronic or accelerated phase CML, 10% had hypophosphatemia, 3—4% had hyperkalemia, 3% had hyponatremia, 1—5% had hypokalemia, and 1—4% had hypocalcemia. Electrolyte abnormalities must be corrected before nilotinib initiation, and electrolyte concentrations should be monitored periodically throughout therapy. Nilotinib is contraindicated for use by patients with either hypokalemia or hypomagnesemia. Also, as nilotinib prolongs the QT interval in a concentration-dependent manner and is metabolized by CYP3A4, avoidance of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors and drugs known to cause QT prolongation is advised (see Drug Interactions). ECGs should be obtained to monitor the QTc at baseline, seven days after initiation, and periodically thereafter including approximately 7 days after any dose adjustment. Nilotinib discontinuation or dose adjustment may be needed for QT prolongation (see Dosage).
 
Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, 31% who received nilotinib had headache; 3% had grade 3 or 4 severity. Among 120 patients with accelerated phase CML, 21% had headache; 2% had grade 3 or 4 severity.
 
Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, who received nilotinib fatigue and asthenia commonly occurred in patients who received nilotinib; 28% and 14%, respectively, of chronic phase CML patients and 16% and 12%, respectively, of accelerated phase CML patients had the events.
 
Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, dizziness, insomnia, and vertigo were noted in 1—10% of patients receiving nilotinib in clinical trials.
 
Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, 11% had peripheral edema, 17% had cough, and 11% had dyspnea. Among 120 patients with accelerated phase CML, 11% had peripheral edema, 13% had cough, and 8% had dyspnea. Dyspnea was of grade 3 or 4 severity in 3% of patients with accelerated phase CML and in 1% of patients with chronic phase CML.
 
Weight gain, weight loss, and dysphonia were noted in 1—10% of patients receiving nilotinib in clinical trials.
 
Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, 33% who received nilotinib had a rash (unspecified). In 2% of patients the rash was of grade 3 or 4 severity. Pruritus occurred in 29% of patients and was of grade 3 or 4 severity in 1%. Fever occurred in 14% of patients and was of grade 3 or 4 severity in 1%. A similar percentage of 120 patients with accelerated phase CML had rash (28%) or pruritus (20%), but none had grade 3 or 4 severity of either side effect. Fever occurred in a greater percentage of accelerated phase CML patients: 24% of 120, and 2% had grade 3 or 4 severity. Eczema, flushing, erythema, alopecia, xerosis, paresthesias, urticaria, hyperhidrosis, and night sweats were noted in 1—10% of patients receiving nilotinib in clinical trials.
 
Among 318 patients with chronic phase CML, 18% who received nilotinib had arthralgia, 14% had myalgia, 13% had pain in extremity, 11% had bone pain, 11% had muscle cramps (spasms), and 10% had back pain. The percentages of patients with accelerated phase CML who had these adverse reactions were similar. Arthralgia, myalgia, pain in extremity, bone pain, muscle spasms, and back pain were of grade 3 or 4 severity in 0—2% of patients. Musculoskeletal pain was noted in 1—10% of patients receiving nilotinib in clinical trials.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    anemia, eosinophilia, leukocytosis, leukopenia, lymphopenia, neutropenia, pancytopenia, thrombocytopenia, thrombocytosis
    General Information
    Myelosuppression including severe anemia, neutropenia, and thrombocytopenia has been reported with nilotinib therapy in clinical trials. Monitor complete blood counts every 2 weeks for the first 2 months of nilotinib treatment and then monthly thereafter or as clinically indicated. Myelosuppression is generally reversible and is managed by holding or dose reducing nilotinib therapy. Anemia (8%; grade 3 or 4, 4%), neutropenia (15%; grade 3 or 4, 12%), and thrombocytopenia (18%; grade 3 or 4, 10%) occurred in patients with newly diagnosed chronic phase (CP) Philadelphia chromosome-positive chronic myelogenous leukemia (Ph+ CML) who received nilotinib in a randomized, comparative trial. Severe hematologic toxicity reported in patients with CP or accelerated phase (AP) Ph+ CML resistant or intolerant to at least 1 prior therapy including imatinib who received nilotinib in a single-arm trial included grade 3 and 4 anemia (CP-CML, 11%; AP-CML, 27%), neutropenia (CP-CML, 31%; AP-CML, 42%), and thrombocytopenia (CP-CML, 30%; AP-CML, 42%); neutropenia, febrile neutropenia, and thrombocytopenia were reported in 1% to 10% of patients. Additionally, eosinophilia, leukopenia, febrile neutropenia, pancytopenia, decreased hemoglobin concentration, and lymphopenia were reported in 1% to 10% of nilotinib-treated patients in pooled result of 2 clinical studies. Single-events of thrombocythemia (thrombocytosis) and leukocytosis have also occurred with nilotinib therapy. In pooled results from 2 studies, decreased total white blood cell count/leukopenia (54%), decreased platelet count/thrombocytopenia (44%), decreased neutrophil count/neutropenia (44%), decreased lymphocyte count/lymphopenia (36%), and decreased hemoglobin level/anemia (38%) were reported in pediatric patients aged 2 to 18 years with CP-CML.

    Headline
    abdominal pain, anorexia, constipation, diarrhea, dysgeusia, dyspepsia, enterocolitis, esophagitis, flatulence, gastritis, gastroesophageal reflux, GI perforation, gingivitis, hemorrhoids, melena, nausea, oral ulceration, peptic ulcer, stomatitis, vomiting, xerostomia
    General Information
    Gastrointestinal (GI) adverse events including nausea (21% to 37%), constipation (20% to 26%), abdominal pain (12% to 18%), and diarrhea (19% to 28%) have been commonly reported with nilotinib therapy in clinical trials. Nausea (22%; grade 3 or 4, 2%), constipation (20%; grade 3 or 4, less than 1%), diarrhea (19%; grade 3 or 4, 1%), upper abdominal pain (18%; grade 3 or 4, 1%), abdominal pain (15%; grade 3 or 4, 2%), vomiting (15%; grade 3 or 4, less than 1%), and dyspepsia (10%) occurred in patients with newly diagnosed chronic phase (CP) Philadelphia chromosome-positive chronic myelogenous leukemia (Ph+ CML) who received nilotinib in a randomized, comparative trial. GI toxicity reported in patients with CP or accelerated phase Ph+ CML resistant or intolerant to at least 1 prior therapy including imatinib who received nilotinib in a single-arm trial included nausea (22% to 37%; grade 3 or 4, 1% or less), constipation (19% to 26%; grade 3 or 4, less than 1%), diarrhea (24% to 28%; grade 3 or 4, 2% to 3%), upper abdominal pain (12% to 14%; grade 3 or 4 less than 1%), abdominal pain (15% to 16%; grade 3 or 4, 2% to 3%), vomiting (13% to 29%; grade 3 or 4, less than 1%), anorexia (15% to 17%; grade 3 or 4, less than 1%), and dyspepsia (4% to 10%; grade 3 or 4, less than 1%). Additionally, abdominal discomfort, abdominal distention, dysgeusia, and flatulence were reported in 1% to 10% of nilotinib-treated patients and oral ulceration, gastroesophageal reflux, stomatitis, esophageal pain, xerostomia, melena, gastritis, and tooth sensitivity were reported in 0.1% to 1% of nilotinib-treated patients in clinical studies. Single-events of oral papilloma, GI perforation, peptic ulcer (gastric ulcer), ulcerative esophagitis, subileus, enterocolitis, hemorrhoids, hiatus hernia, and gingivitis have also occurred with nilotinib therapy. In pooled results from 2 studies, nausea and vomiting were reported in greater than 20% of pediatric patients aged 2 to 18 years with CP-CML.

    Headline
    QT prolongation
    General Information
    QT prolongation (on electrocardiogram [ECG]) has been reported in 1% to 10% of nilotinib-treated patients in clinical studies. Additionally, sudden cardiac death (possibly related to prolonged cardiac ventricular repolarization) occurred in 0.3% of patients who received nilotinib in clinical trials. Obtain an electrocardiogram at baseline, after 7 days on therapy, after dose adjustments, and periodically as clinically indicated. Hold nilotinib therapy in patients who develop a QTc greater than 480 milliseconds (msec); additionally, a nilotinib dosage adjustment and/or therapy discontinuation may be necessary. An increased QTcF greater than 60 msec from baseline occurred in 1 patient (0.4%) with newly diagnosed chronic phase (CP) Philadelphia chromosome-positive chronic myelogenous leukemia (Ph+CML) who received nilotinib in a randomized, comparative trial. An increased QTcF greater than 60 msec from baseline (4.1%) and a QTcF greater than 500 msec (less than 1%) were reported in patients with CP or accelerated phase Ph+CML resistant or intolerant to at least 1 prior therapy including imatinib who received nilotinib in a single-arm trial; the maximum QTcF change from baseline was 10 msec in this study. Concentration-dependent QT interval prolongation was observed in healthy patients in a placebo-controlled study; the maximum mean placebo-adjusted QTcF change from baseline was 18 msec. Of note, the peak plasma nilotinib concentrations were 26% lower in this study compared with concentrations in the single-arm clinical trial. An increase in QTcF greater than 30 msec from baseline was observed in 28% of pediatric patients aged 2 to 18 years with CP-Ph+CML in pooled results from 2 studies. No patient had an absolute QTcF increase of greater than 500 msec or a QTcF increase of greater than 60 msec from baseline.

    Headline
    ascites, edema, fluid retention, pericardial effusion, peripheral edema, pleural effusion, pulmonary edema
    General Information
    Pleural effusion, pericardial effusion, ascites, and pulmonary edema have been reported with nilotinib therapy in patients with chronic myelogenous leukemia (CML). Monitor patients for signs of fluid retention (e.g., rapid weight gain or swelling) and symptoms of respiratory or cardiac compromise (e.g., shortness of breath). Manage patients with fluid retention or edema as medically indicated. In patients with newly diagnosed chronic phase (CP), Philadelphia chromosome-positive (Ph+) CML who received nilotinib, grade 3/4 fluid retention (2.9% to 3.9%) and effusions, ascites, or pulmonary edema (1.1% to 2.2%; grade 3 or 4, 0.4% to 0.7%) were reported in a clinical trial. Peripheral edema occurred in 9% (grade 3 or 4, less than 1%) of patients in this trial. Peripheral edema was reported in 12% to 15% (grade 3 or 4, less than 1%) of patients with CP-CML and accelerated phase CML who were resistant or intolerant to at least 1 prior therapy including imatinib and who received nilotinib in a single-arm trial. In a pooled analysis of 2 clinical studies, gravitational edema, plural effusions, pericardial effusions, and pulmonary edema were each reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients who received nilotinib. A single case of localized edema has also occurred with nilotinib therapy.

    Headline
    angina, atrial fibrillation, atrial flutter, AV block, bradycardia, chest pain (unspecified), cyanosis, heart failure, hypertension, hypertensive crisis, hypotension, myocardial infarction, palpitations, pericarditis, sinus tachycardia, thrombosis, thrombotic microangiopathy
    General Information
    Cardiotoxicity has been reported with nilotinib therapy in clinical trials. Hypertension occurred in 10% (grade 3 or 4, 1%) of patients with newly diagnosed chronic phase (CP) Philadelphia chromosome-positive chronic myelogenous leukemia (Ph+CML) who received nilotinib in a randomized, comparative trial. In this trial, arterial vascular occlusive events (9% to 15%), ischemic heart disease-related events (5% to 5.8%), peripheral arterial occlusive disease (2.9% to 3.6%), and ischemic cerebrovascular events (1.4% to 3.2%) were reported. Hypertension was reported in 10% to 11% (grade 3 or 4, less than 1% to 2%) of patients with CP-Ph+CML and accelerated phase Ph+CML who were resistant or intolerant to at least 1 prior therapy including imatinib and who received nilotinib in a single-arm trial. Additionally, chest pain (unspecified), chest discomfort, hypertension, angina pectoris, AV block, atrial flutter, extrasystoles, atrial fibrillation, sinus tachycardia, bradycardia, and palpitations were reported in 1% to 10% of nilotinib-treated patients and hypertensive crisis, peripheral arterial occlusive disease, intermittent claudication, arterial stenosis limb, arteriosclerosis, heart failure, coronary artery disease, cyanosis, myocardial infarction, myocardial ischemia, coronary artery stenosis, and cardiac murmur were reported in 0.1% to 1% of nilotinib-treated patients in clinical studies. Single-events of arteriosclerosis, increased troponin level, ventricular dysfunction, pericarditis, decreased ejection fraction, hemorrhagic shock, hypotension, and thrombosis have also occurred with nilotinib therapy. Thrombotic microangiopathy has been reported during postmarketing surveillance.
